Quasi-coherent sheaves and 𝒟 \mathcal{D} -modules in Derived Differential Supergeometry
David Carchedi

Abstract
. Derived geometry provides powerful tools to handle non-transverse intersections and singular moduli problems arising in geometry and theoretical physics. While derived algebraic geometry has been extensively developed, classical field theories—formulated as variational problems involving sections of smooth fiber bundles over manifolds—naturally require the language of differential geometry, infinite-dimensional analysis (e.g., Fréchet manifolds), and additional geometric structures on spacetime, such as smooth metrics. Moreover, field theories incorporating fermionic matter fields necessitate extending the framework to include supermanifolds. This article is the first in a sequence aimed at rigorously modeling the derived space of solutions to the field equations of Lagrangian gauge theories as derived 𝐂 ∞ \mathbf{C}^{\infty} -stacks. While this article does not explicitly discuss physics or field theory, it develops foundational aspects of derived differential geometry which are useful in their own right and contribute to the further development of the field. Moreover, these results provide essential groundwork for subsequent papers rigorously constructing derived spaces of solutions to Euler-Lagrange equations. We establish foundational results extending existing work on derived manifolds into supergeometric and infinite-dimensional contexts, and explicitly relate these constructions to differential operators and PDE theory. This paper an excerpt from a larger manuscript currently in preparation and is made available now to disseminate key foundational developments. We begin by extending key results about derived manifolds to the supergeometric context, explicitly defining derived supermanifolds. This generalization is crucial for subsequent articles addressing fermionic matter fields. We also carefully clarify the role of infinite-dimensional manifolds in derived 𝐂 ∞ \mathbf{C}^{\infty} -geometry, providing two different embeddings of the category of convenient manifolds of Frölicher-Kriegl-Michor into derived stacks, expanding on work of Steffens. Moreover, we develop a novel approach to quasi-coherent sheaves, characterizing them as an explicit full subcategory of sheaves of modules on the large site. This formulation provides a concrete and explicit description of quasi-coherent sheaves over general stacks, in contrast to the traditional abstract definition as a limit of ∞ \infty -categories arising from an affine cover. It is particularly well-suited to studying examples such as de Rham stacks, which provide a natural geometric setting for analyzing PDEs. In this direction, we introduce a topos-theoretic definition of infinite jet bundles and nonlinear differential operators via the de Rham stack, and prove that this construction recovers the classical infinite jet bundle of a fiber bundle, including its Fréchet manifold structure, extending work of Khavkine-Schreiber. Finally, we establish an equivalence between the ∞ \infty -category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the de Rham stack ℳ d ​ R \mathcal{M}_{dR} of any supermanifold ℳ \mathcal{M} and the ∞ \infty -category of 𝒟 ℳ \mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}} -modules, giving a direct link to linear PDEs. Due to fundamental differences from the algebraic setting—such as the lack of compact generation, the fact that all module sheaves over affine 𝐂 ∞ \mathbf{C}^{\infty} -schemes are quasi-coherent, and that quasi-coherent sheaves form a full subcategory of module sheaves rather than vice versa—this equivalence does not formally follow from analogous results in derived algebraic geometry.



1.
Introduction

Given two submanifolds
X
and
Y
of a smooth manifold
M,
if they intersect transversely,
X\times_{M}Y
is a closed submanifold. However, without transversality, things go terribly wrong; an arbitrary closed subset can be obtained this way. Even if the intersection is a manifold, it often does not have the expected cohomological properties, e.g. in cobordism theory. The theory of
Derived Differential Geometry
(DDG) is designed to fix these deficiencies and is analogous to DAG. Such a theory provides a geometric framework in which arbitrary pullbacks of smooth maps between smooth manifolds exist as smooth objects that behave well with respect to cohomology, and agree with the ordinary fibered product in the case the maps are transverse. Objects which can be constructed iteratively in this manner are called
derived manifolds
.

There are various approaches to derived
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-geometry,
[
33
,
16
,
6
,
9
,
14
,
11
,
3
,
34
,
26
]
, but a common theme is, in some way shape or form, derived
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-geometry is derived
algebraic
geometry over
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-rings.
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-rings are commutative
\mathbb{R}
-algebras with extra structure, that allow one to not only interpret polynomials, but also smooth functions. The prototypical example is the ring
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(M\right)
of smooth functions on a manifold.
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-rings are algebras for a Lawvere theory, and as such there is well established way of discussing derived versions of such algebras as simplicial
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-rings. Roytenberg and the author offered an alternative equivalent differential graded approach using dg-
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras
[
9
]
, and many foundational results about the derived algebraic geometry of such algebras was developed by Nuiten in their thesis
[
14
]
, and developed even further by Steffens
[
34
]
. Together with Steffens, we defined derived manifolds via a universal property
[
11
]
and proved they were equivalent to the affine derived schemes of finite type for the algebraic geometry of
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-rings. In subsequent work, we went on to prove that there is an equivalent description in terms of differential graded manifolds
[
8
]
.

In this article, we define derived supermanifolds in an analogous way as in our work with Steffens, and carefully extend the above two results to this setting. To do so, we use the
2
-sorted algebraic theory of
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-rings we developed with Roytenberg
[
10
,
9
]
. We also clarify recent work of Steffens related to the integration of derived geometry with the convenient manifolds of Frölicher-Kriegl-Michor.

We go on two develop a theory of modules and quasi-coherent sheaves. This definition is the same as that of Nuiten
[
14
]
and Steffens
[
34
]
, but we present a novel approach which is particularly convenient for working with derived stacks which are not affine
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-schemes. Our approach allows one to view quasi-coherent sheaves over
\mathcal{X}
concretely as certain
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-module sheaves over the
big
site of
\mathcal{X},
and is based in part on key ideas from
[
1
]
.

We then go on to discuss infinite-jet bundles and
\mathcal{D}
-modules in this context. Both of these are discussed in terms of Simpson’s
de Rham stack

[
32
]
. The case of infinite-jet bundles largely follows work of Khavkine-Schreiber
[
17
]
carefully adapted to the derived setting. We first show that the infinite jet bundle of any fiber bundle over a manifold, complete with its Frechét manifold structure, can be reconstructed purely from the de Rham stack. We then study quasi-coherent sheaves over the de Rham stack. Our main result is that for any supermanifold
\mathcal{M},
there is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories between
\mathcal{D}
-modules over
\mathcal{M}
and quasi-coherent sheaves over the de Rham stack
\mathcal{M}_{dR}.
This mirrors classical equivalences established by Gaitsgory and Rozenblyum
[
13
]
, but the method of proof is quite different. On one hand, there is a technical challenge that
\mathbf{QC}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
is rarely compactly generated, and there is no good notion of coherent sheaf, making approaches based on ind-coherent sheaves difficult to apply. On the other hand, our framework naturally takes into account the geometry of jets in that the notion of differential operators can be formulated in terms of the Frechét manifold structure on the infinite jet bundle.

The field of derived differential is under rapid development, but there is still much to be done. One striking omission from this article is a reformulation in the language of dg-manifolds. This is work in progress.

1.1.
Organization and Main Results:

In Section
2
, we briefly review the general theory of multi-sorted algebraic theories and their homotopy algebras. The main example of interest is the
2
-sorted theory of
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebras of
[
10
,
9
]
.

In Section
3
, we summarize important basic properties of
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras and extend them to
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebras.

In Section
4
we review the universal property of derived manifolds from
[
11
]
, and its relationship with
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras. We also extend this definition to supermanifolds in the obvious way:

Definition 1.1
.

(Definition
4.5
)
The
\infty
-category
\mathsf{DSMfd}
of derived supermanifolds is the (essentially unique)
\infty
-category with finite limits, together with a functor
i:\mathsf{SMfd}\to\mathsf{DSMfd}
which preserves transverse pullbacks and the terminal object, such that for all
\infty
-categories
\mathscr{C}
with finite limits, composition along
i
induces an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\operatorname{Fun}^{\mathbf{lex}}\left(\mathsf{DSMfd},\mathscr{C}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Fun}^{\pitchfork}\left(\mathsf{SMfd},\mathscr{C}\right)

between the
\infty
-category of finite limit preserving functors from
\mathsf{DSMfd}
to
\mathscr{C}
and the
\infty
-category of functors from
\mathsf{SMfd}
to
\mathscr{C}
which preserves transverse pullbacks and the terminal object.

In Section
5
, we discuss the homotopy theory and homological algebra of homotopical
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebras. We review the model structure on dg-
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-superalgebras, discuss the localization of such algebras, and the construction of graded Koszul complexes. As an application, we prove that the morphism of algebraic theories

\mathbf{SCom}_{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}

is unramified, and use this to prove:

Corollary 1.2
.

(Corollary
4.7
)
There is a canonical equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathsf{DSMfd}\simeq\left(\mathbf{Alg}_{{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{\mathbf{fp}}\right)^{op}

between the
\infty
-category of derived supermanifolds and the opposite of the
\infty
-category of finitely presented
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebras in
\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}.

In Section
6
we discuss the spectrum functor
\mathbf{Spec}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}
for
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebras, and define derived
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-schemes, and prove that for a supermanifold
\mathcal{M},

\mathcal{M}\simeq\mathbf{Spec}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\right),
as a locally ringed space. In Section
6.1
we explore the link between infinite-dimensional manifolds and
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-schemes. In particular, we prove:

Proposition 1.3
.

(Proposition
6.34
)
For a locally convex vector space
E
or convenient vector space,
\left(E,\mathbf{C}^{\infty}_{E}\right)
is an affine
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-scheme if and only if
E
smoothly regular, realcompact, and every germ of a
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-function has a global representative. Similarly, for
E
a convenient vector space,
\left(E_{c^{\infty}},\mathbf{C}^{\infty}_{C}\right)
is an affine
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-scheme if and only if
E_{c^{\infty}}
smoothly regular and realcompact, where
E_{c^{\infty}}
is
E
equipped with the
c^{\infty}
-topology.

which has the following corollary:

Corollary 1.4
.

(Corollary
6.35
)
The following classes of locally convex or convenient vector spaces are affine
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-schemes when equipped with their appropriate sheaf of smooth functions:

1.

Any smoothly regular locally convex vector space which admits smooth partitions of unity. This includes:

a)

Any metrizable locally convex vector space for which smooth functions separate disjoint closed sets.

b)

All Hilbert spaces.

c)

All Nuclear Frechét spaces.

2.

All realcompact smoothly regular convenient vector spaces (with the
c^{\infty}
-topology).

3.

Any smoothly regular locally convex vector space which is Lindelöf. Any smoothly regular convenient vector space which is Lindelöf for its
c^{\infty}
-topology.

We show that nonetheless, the induced functor from locally convex vector spaces to
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-schemes is only faithful and not full unless restricted to convenient vector spaces. We also examine the fully faithful inclusion of convenient vector spaces into the topos of sheaves on affine
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-schemes induced by first taking their functor of points on finite dimensional manifolds, and then left Kan extending. We show that for a manifold
M
, these two embeddings agree if and only if
M
is finite dimensional. Finally, we prove that the infinite jet bundle of a submersion over a finite dimensional manifold
M,
when regarded as a
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-scheme, is the limit in
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-schemes of the associated finite jet-bundles.

In Section
7
, we discuss various adjunctions that arise on the
\infty
-topos of sheaves over a suitably chosen site of affine
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-schemes, in the spirit of Schreiber’s differential cohesion
[
29
]
. We emphasize that the
\infty
-topoi arising as sheaves over small sites of affine
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-schemes are
NOT
cohesive however.

In Section
8
, we specialize our theory of higher orbifolds and Deligne-Mumford stacks
[
7
]
to the derived
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-setting. We establish some technical results we use later in our study of quasi-coherent sheaves.

In Section
9
, we define modules and quasi-coherent sheaves in the setting of derived
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-geometry. Our first result is the following:

Theorem 1.5
.

(Theorem
9.3
)
For any dg-
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-superalgebra
\mathcal{A},
there is a canonical equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathbf{d}{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}\mathbf{Alg}/\mathcal{A}\right)\simeq\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{A}}

between the stabilization of the slice category over
\mathcal{A},
and the
\infty
-category of unbounded dg-modules over
\mathcal{A}.

We go on to discuss square-zero extensions, derivations, and tangent
\infty
-categories. In Sections
9.4
and
9.5
, we define module sheaves for
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-ringed
\infty
-topoi and use their functoriality properties to give a very general version of a module-spectrum functor.

In Section
9.6
, we develop the theory of quasi-coherent sheaves. In
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-geometry, the theory of quasi-coherent sheaves is a bit different than that of algebraic geometry, as was already noticed by Joyce
[
15
]
. For
X=\left(\mathbf{Spec}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right),\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{A}}\right)
an affine
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-scheme,
every

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{A}}
-module is quasi-coherent, but not every
\mathcal{A}
-module is global sections of an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{A}}
-module. Quasi-coherent sheaves on
\mathbf{Spec}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
are equivalent to a reflective subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
-modules called
complete

\mathcal{A}
-modules
\widehat{\mathbf{Mod}}_{\mathcal{A}}
. It follows that it is a symmetric monoidal with an inherited tensor product
\widehat{\otimes}
from that of
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathbf{Spec}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}.
Whereas our definition of quasi-coherent sheaf agrees with that of Nuiten
[
14
]
and Steffens
[
34
]
, we present a novel approach which is particularly convenient for working with derived stacks which are not affine
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-schemes. Our approach allows one to view quasi-coherent sheaves over
\mathcal{X}
concretely as certain
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-module sheaves over the
big
site of
\mathcal{X},
and is based in part on key ideas from
[
1
]
. Denoting
\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}
the
\infty
-topos of sheaves on a small site of affine
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-schemes, the pair
\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}},\mathbb{R}\right)
is a
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-ringed
\infty
-topos, and we denote
\mathbb{R}
when regarded as a
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-ring object as
\mathcal{O}.
If
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}
is any stack,
\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}/\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)
is an
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-ringed
\infty
-topos. We prove:

Proposition 1.6
.

(Proposition
9.58
)
For any stack
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}},

\mathbf{QC}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
can be realized as a coreflective subcategory of
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}
.

Moreover, for any quasi-coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on derived stack
\mathcal{X}
, realized as an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-module, and any morphism
f:\mathbf{Spec}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right),

\mathcal{F}\left(f\right)
is a
complete

\mathcal{A}
-module and we have the following theorem:

Theorem 1.7
.

(Theorem
9.63
)
Let
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}
be an arbitrary derived stack. Then the following conditions are equivalent for an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-module
\mathcal{F}
:

i)

\mathcal{F}
is quasi-coherent (i.e. in the essential image of the fully-faithful functor
\Lambda^{\star}_{\mathcal{X}}
).

ii)

For all morphisms

\textstyle{\mathbf{Spec}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{B}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Spec}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}}

the canonical map

\mathcal{B}\underset{\mathcal{A}}{\widehat{\otimes}}\mathcal{F}\left(f\right)\to\mathcal{F}\left(g\right)

is an equivalence.

In Section
10
, we introduce the de Rham stack in the derived
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-setting. We use it to define formal neighborhoods, and the notions of formally étale and formal smoothness.

In Section
11
, we review the concept of
\infty
-group object introduced by Nikolaus-Schreiber-Stevenson in
[
36
]
. There are no new results here.

In Section
12
we introduce jet bundles and differential operators synthetically in terms of the de Rham stack. The main definitions are an adaption of ideas and concepts from
[
17
]
of Khavkine-Schrieber. Given any derived stack
\mathcal{X},
it comes with a canonical map

\eta:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}_{dR},

to its de Rham stack, which equivalently is a geometric morphism between their slice topoi

\eta:\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}/cX\to\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}/\mathcal{X}_{dR}.

Given an object
\pi:E\to\mathcal{X}
in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}/\mathcal{X},
which one can regard as a generalized bundle, the
infinite jet bundle
of
E
is
\mathcal{J}^{\infty}E:=\eta^{*}\eta_{*}E.
(or more precisely
\eta^{*}\eta_{*}\pi
). To justify this definition, we prove:

Theorem 1.8
.

(Corollary
12.15
)
Let
\pi:E\to M
be a fiber bundle over a finite dimensional manifold. Then
\mathcal{J}^{\infty}E
is the usual infinite-jet bundle with its Frechét manifold structure, regarded as a
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-scheme.

(A similar result in the non-derived context was proven in
[
17
]
.) We then discuss the notion of non-linear differential operators in this context and how they induce morphisms between respective stacks of sections. The
\infty
-jet functor
\mathcal{J}^{\infty}
has the canonical structure of a comonad, and this closely related to Vinogradov’s category of formally integrable PDEs
[
38
,
5
]
. The linearized version is related to
\mathcal{D}
-modules.

In Section
13
we start by discussing the general theory of monads and comonads and their interaction with higher topos theory. Along the way we prove the
\infty
-categorical analogue of a well-known result in classical category theory:

Lemma 1.9
.

(Lemma
13.3
)
Given a monad
T
on an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C},
there is a pullback diagram of
\infty
-categories

\textstyle{\mathbf{Alg}_{T}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Psh\left(\mathbf{Kl}\left(T\right)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(F_{\mathbf{Kl}}\right)^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{\Psh\left(\mathscr{C}\right),}

where
\mathbf{Kl}\left(T\right)
is the Kleisli
\infty
-category of free
T
-algebras.

As an application we prove the following:

Corollary 1.10
.

(Corollary
13.12
)
Let
f:E\to F
be an epimorphism in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}
. Then the following diagram of
\infty
-categories is a pullback:

\textstyle{\mathbf{QC}\left(F\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda^{\star}\left(F\right)}
\scriptstyle{f^{\star}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\star}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{QC}\left(E\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda^{\star}\left(E\right)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}.}

I.e. one can identify quasi-coherent sheaves on
\mathbf{QC}\left(F\right)
as those
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-modules
\mathcal{F}
whose pullback
f^{\star}\mathcal{F}
to
F
are quasi-coherent.

The rest of Section
13
contains a series of technical
\infty
-categorical results necessary to prove the main result about
\mathcal{D}
-modules.

Section
14
is dedicated to the theory of
\mathcal{D}
-modules. The
\infty
-jet comonad
\mathcal{J}^{\infty}
canonically lifts to a comonad on
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}
allowing one to study linear differential operators systematically, and to define the ring of differential operators abstractly in such a way as to recover the usual ring in the case of a manifold or supermanifold:

Proposition 1.11
.

(Propositions
14.9
and
14.11
)
Let
\mathcal{M}
be a supermanifold. Then
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
is the sheaf which assigns each open subset
U
of the core
|\mathcal{M}|_{0}
the ring of linear differential operators of
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{M}}\left(U\right)
which are
locally
of finite order. In particular, for
M
an even manifold, such differential operators are the same as maps of Frechét vector bundles
\mathcal{J}^{\infty}M\to\underline{\mathbb{R}}.

Finally, we prove the main result:

Theorem 1.12
.

(Theorem
14.15
)
Let
\mathcal{M}
be a supermanifold. Then there is a canonical equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathbf{QC}\left(\mathcal{M}_{dR}\right)\simeq\mathcal{D}\mbox{-}\mathbf{Mod}\left(\mathcal{M}\right).

2.
Algebraic theories

Definition 2.1
.

An
algebraic theory
is an
\infty
-category
\mathbf{T}
with finite products. A morphism of algebraic theories

\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{T}^{\prime}

is a finite product preserving functor. Denote the associated
\infty
-category by
\mathbf{AlgThy}.

Example 2.2
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
be a set. Regarding this set as a discrete category, let
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbb{S}}
be the
free completion of
\mathbb{S}
with respect to finite products. Concretely, the objects are finite families

\left(s_{i}\in\mathbb{S}\right)_{i\in I}

and morphisms

\left(s_{i}\in\mathbb{S}\right)_{i\in I}\to\left(t_{j}\in\mathbb{S}\right)_{j\in J}

are functions of finite sets
f:J\to I
such that

s_{f\left(j\right)}=t_{j}

for all
j\in J
.
Then
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbb{S}}
is the
algebraic theory of
\mathbb{S}
-sorts
.

If
\mathbb{S}
is a singleton set, then
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbb{S}}\cong\mathbf{FinSet}^{op}
is the opposite category of finite sets. We denote this algebraic theory by
\mathbf{T}_{obj}.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an
\infty
-category with finite products. The
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)
of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras in
\mathscr{C}
is the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathbf{T},\mathscr{C}\right)
on those functors which preserve finite products.

Of particular importance is the cases when
\mathscr{C}=\mathrm{Set}
and when
\mathscr{C}=\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}
. We will call an object of
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)
a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra, and an object of
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathrm{Set}\right)
a
0
-truncated
\mathbf{T}
-algebra.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a presentable
\infty
-category. Then
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)
is also presentable.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
[
22
, Lemmas 5.5.4.17, 5.5.4.18, 5.5.4.19]
.
∎

Example 2.5
.

For any
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
with finite products, there is a canonical equivalence
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}_{obj}.}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\simeq\mathscr{C}
given by evaluation at
*
. More generally, for any set
\mathbb{S},
there is a canonical equivalence
\mathbf{Alg}_{T_{\mathbb{S}}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\simeq\mathscr{C}^{\mathbb{S}}.
For example, if
\mathbb{S}=\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\},
a
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbb{S}}
-algebra in
\mathscr{C}
is the same data as the choice of
n
objects of
\mathscr{C}.

Definition 2.6
.

Given an algebraic theory
\mathbf{T},
an object
r\in\mathbf{T}_{0}
is called a
generator
if every object in
\mathbf{T}
is equivalent to
r^{n},
for some
n\geq 0.
More generally, a subset
\mathbb{S}\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{0}
is said to be a set of generators for
\mathbf{T}
if every object in
\mathbf{T}
is equivalent to a finite product of objects in
\mathbb{S}.

Definition 2.7
.

An
\mathbb{S}
-sorted Lawvere theory
is a skeletal algebraic theory
\mathbf{T}
together with a chosen a set of generators
\mathbb{S}\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{0}.
Equivalently, this is the same as a faithful map of algebraic theories

\sigma_{\mathbf{T}}:\mathbf{T}_{\mathbb{S}}\to\mathbf{T}.

A morphism
\varphi:\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{S}
-sorted Lawvere theories is a morphism in
\mathbb{S}/\mathbf{AlgThy},
i.e. a finite product preserving functor making the following diagram commute

\textstyle{\mathbf{T}_{\mathbb{S}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{\mathbf{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}.}

Remark 2.8
.

Since
\mathbf{T}
is further assumed skeletal, we may assume, without loss of generality, that the set of objects of
\mathbf{T}
is
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{S}}.
Often, the set
\mathbb{S}
is assumed to be finite. When
\mathbb{S}
has
n
elements, the Lawvere theory is said to be
n
-sorted. In this paper, we will only need to discuss
1
-sorted and
2
-sorted Lawvere theories.

Most types of algebraic objects have an associated algebraic theory. For example:

Example 2.9
.

Let
k
be a commutative ring and let
\mathbf{Com}_{k}
be the the opposite category of finitely generated free
k
-algebras. Making this a skeletal category, we may represent it as the category of affine
k
-schemes of the form
\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n},
for
n\geq 0.
Then,
\mathbf{Com}_{k}
is a
1
-sorted Lawvere theory with
\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}
as a generator.

A
\mathbf{Com}_{k}
-algebra in
\mathrm{Set}
is an ordinary commutative
k
-algebra. More precisely, given a
\mathbf{Com}_{k}
-algebra

\mathcal{A}:\mathbf{Com}_{k}\to\mathrm{Set},

the set
\mathcal{A}\left(\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}\right)
has an induced structure of a commutative
k
-algebra. This is because
\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}
is a commutative
k
-algebra object in schemes (and hence in
\mathbf{Com}_{k}
) and any finite product preserving functor preserves commutative
k
-algebra objects. For example, the maps

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}=\mathbb{A}_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}

\displaystyle\left(x,y\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle x+y

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}=\mathbb{A}_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}

\displaystyle\left(x,y\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle x\cdot y

define addition and multiplication for the ring object
\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}.
Conversely, given a commutative
k
-algebra
B,
the functor

\mathbb{H}\mathrm{om}\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu},B\right)=\mathbb{H}\mathrm{om}\left(\mathbf{Spec}\,\left(B\right),\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)

preserves finite products, hence is a
\mathbf{Com}_{k}
-algebra in
\mathrm{Set}
. These constructions are categorically inverse to one another.

Example 2.10
.

Similarly, let
k
be a commutative ring and let
\mathbf{SCom}_{k}
be the the opposite category of supercommutative
k
-algebras free on finitely many even and odd generators. This can be identified with the category of superschemes over
\mathbf{Spec}\,\left(k\right)
of the form
\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n|m},
for
n,m\geq 0.
The category
\mathbf{SCom}_{k}
is a
2
-sorted Lawvere theory with two generators;
\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}=\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1|0}
is the
even
generator, and

\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1}=\mathbb{A}_{k}^{0|1}=\mathbf{Spec}\,\left(k\left[\theta\right]\right),

(with
\theta
an element of degree
1
) is the
odd
generator.

Similarly to above, the category of
\mathbf{SCom}_{k}
-algebras in
\mathrm{Set}
is canonically isomorphic to the category of supercommutative
k
-algebras. In slightly more detail,
a
\mathbf{SCom}_{k}
-algebra

\mathcal{A}:\mathbf{SCom}_{k}\to\mathrm{Set},

determines a supercommutative algebra whose set of even elements is
\mathcal{A}\left(\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1|0}\right)
and whose set of odd elements is
\mathcal{A}\left(\mathbb{A}_{k}^{0|1}\right).
From this point of a view, a supercommutative algebra does not have an underlying set, but instead has an underlying
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded set.

This works well for other types of algebraic gadgets, such as groups, monoids, abelian groups, modules etc.

Example 2.11
.

Denote by
\mathbf{T}_{\mathrm{Ab}}
the opposite of the full subcategory of abelian groups on those of the form
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
for some
n.
Then
\mathbf{T}_{\mathrm{Ab}}
is an algebraic theory with
\mathbb{Z}
as a generator. A classical
\mathbf{T}_{\mathrm{Ab}}
algebra is an abelian group.

Moreover, if
\mathscr{C}
is any category, or
\infty
-category, with finite products, then
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}_{\mathrm{Ab}}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)
is the category of abelian group objects in
\mathscr{C},
and hence we write

\mathrm{Ab}\left(\mathscr{C}\right):=\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}_{\mathrm{Ab}}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)

More generally, the above holds with the category of abelian groups (
\mathbb{Z}
-modules) replaced with modules for any commutative ring
k
, resulting in an algebraic theory
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{Mod}_{k}}.

The following two examples are of fundamental importance for derived
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-geometry:

Example 2.12
.

Let
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
denote the full subcategory of the category of smooth manifolds
\mathsf{Mfd}
on those manifolds of the form
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n.
Then
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
is an algebraic theory with
\mathbb{R}
as a generator.
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras in
\mathrm{Set}
are also known as
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-rings.

Note that if
k=\mathbb{R},

\mathbf{Com}_{\mathbb{R}}
may also be seen as the category of manifolds of the form
\mathbb{R}^{n}
whose morphisms are given by polynomials. Hence, there is an evident functor
\mathbf{Com}_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
which is a morphism of
1
-sorted Lawvere theories.

Example 2.13
.

Similarly, let
\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}
denote the full subcategory of the category of supermanifolds
\mathsf{SMfd}
on those of the form
\mathbb{R}^{p|q}
for
p,q\geq 0.
Then
\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}
is a
2
-sorted Lawvere theory with the real line
\mathbb{R}^{1}=\mathbb{R}^{1|0}
as the even generator, and the odd line
\mathbb{R}^{0|1}
as the odd generator.
\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}
-algebras in
\mathrm{Set}
are introduced in
[
10
]
and are also known as
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-superalgebras
.

Similarly to above, there is an evident morphism of
2
-sorted Lawvere theories

\mathbf{SCom}_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}.

We will review some of the important aspects of (super)
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras in Section
3
.

2.1.
Categorical properties of algebraic theories

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an algebraic theory. Then as any co-representable functor preserves finite products, the Yoneda embedding restricts to a fully faithful functor
j:\mathbf{T}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{op}.

Theorem 2.14
.

[
22
, Proposition 5.5.8.10]

a)

\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)
is a localization of
\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathbf{T},\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right).

b)

The
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)
is compactly generated. In particular,

\operatorname{Ind}\left(\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{\mathbf{fp}}\right)\simeq\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right),

where
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{\mathbf{fp}}
denotes the compact objects.

c)

The inclusion
j:\mathbf{T}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{op}
preserves finite products.

The functor
j
also has the following universal property:

Theorem 2.15
.

[
22
, Proposition 5.5.8.15]

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an
\infty
-category which admits sifted limits. Then, composition with
j
induces an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\operatorname{Fun}^{sift}\left(\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{op},\mathscr{C}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathbf{T},\mathscr{C}\right),

between the
\infty
-category of functors
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{op}\to\mathscr{C}
which preserves sifted limits, to the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathbf{T},\mathscr{C}\right),
and if
\mathscr{C}
is complete, it also induces an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\operatorname{Fun}^{R}\left(\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{op},\mathscr{C}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Fun}^{\Pi\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu}\left(\mathbf{T},\mathscr{C}\right)=\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)

between the
\infty
-category of functors
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{op}\to\mathscr{C}
which preserves small limits, and the
\infty
-category of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras in
\mathscr{C}.
The inverse of both equivalences is given by right Kan extension.

We have the following corollary:

Corollary 2.16
.

A functor
F:\mathbf{T}\to\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}
is a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra if and only if
F
is a sifted colimit of corepresentable functors

F\simeq\underset{\alpha}{\underrightarrow{\mbox{colim}\vskip 0.5pt}\mspace{4mu}}j\left(t_{\alpha}\right).

Lemma 2.17
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is presentable, then
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)
is reflective in
\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathbf{T},\mathscr{C}\right).

Proof.

Since both
\infty
-categories are presentable, by the adjoint functor theorem, it suffices to prove that the inclusion preserves limits. But this follows immediately from the fact that limits commute with finite products.
∎

By an analogous proof, we also have:

Lemma 2.18
.

For any
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
in which sifted colimits commute with finite products (e.g. an
\infty
-topos, or more generally any
\infty
-category monadic over an
\infty
-topos), then inclusion

\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathbf{T},\mathscr{C}\right)

preserves sifted colimits.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a presentable
\infty
-category in which sifted colimits commute with finite products (e.g. an
\infty
-topos, or more generally any
\infty
-category monadic over an
\infty
-topos), and suppose that
\mathbf{T}
is an
\mathbb{S}
-sorted Lawvere theory with
\mathbb{S}
finite. Then
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)
is monadic over
\mathscr{C}^{\mathbb{S}}.

Proof.

Since both
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)
are presentable, it suffices to prove that the functor

R:\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\to\mathscr{C}^{\mathbb{S}}

given by
R=\underset{s\in\mathbb{S}}{\prod}\mathbf{ev}_{s},
preserves limits and sifted colimits, and is conservative. It is clearly conservative. Notice that
R
factors as:

\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathbf{T},\mathscr{C}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\underset{s\in\mathbb{S}}{\prod}\mathbf{ev}_{s}}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{C}^{\mathbb{S}}.

The first functor preserves limits by Lemma
2.17
, and the second since limits in functor categories are computed object-wise. The first functor preserves sifted colimits by Proposition
2.18
, and the second does since colimits in the functor category are computed object-wise.
∎

Definition 2.20
.

Let
\mathbf{T}
be an
\mathbb{S}
-sorted Lawvere theory. Denote the left adjoint to

\underset{s\in\mathbb{S}}{\prod}\mathbf{ev}_{s}:\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)\to\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}^{\mathbb{S}}

by

\mathbf{T}\left\{\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right\}:\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}^{\mathbb{S}}\to\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right).

\mathbf{T}
-algebras in the essential image of
\mathbf{T}\left\{\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right\}
are called
free
\mathbf{T}
-algebras
.

Remark 2.21
.

The
\mathbf{T}
-algebras in the essential image of
j:\mathbf{T}^{op}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)
are also in the essential image of
\mathbf{T}\left\{\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right\}
; they are precisely the
finitely generated
free
\mathbf{T}
-algebras. For example, if
\mathbf{T}
is
1
-sorted, with generator
r,
then
j\left(r^{n}\right)\simeq\mathbf{T}\left\{\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}\right\}
is the free algebras on
n
generators. We usually denote this as
\mathbf{T}\left\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\right\}.

Example 2.22
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{T}
is a
2
-sorted Lawvere theory, with generators
r,s\in\mathbf{T}.
We cannot speak of the free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra on
n
-generators, since
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathrm{Set}\right)
is not monadic over
\mathrm{Set},
but rather over
\mathrm{Set}^{2}.
But we can speak of the free
\mathbf{T}
-algebra on
n
generators of
type
r
and
m
-generators of
type
s
, we denote it by
\mathbf{T}\left\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n};\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots\eta_{m}\right\}.
The most important example for this paper will be
\mathbf{T}={\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}},
the
2
-sorted Lawvere theory of super
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras, of Example
2.13
. We call the sort corresponding to the generator
\mathbb{R}

even
and the sort corresponding to the generator
\mathbb{R}^{0|1}

odd
. We denote the free
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebra on
n
even and
m
odd generators by
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots x_{n};\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\ldots\eta_{m}\right\}.
It the
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebra of smooth functions on the supermanifold
\mathbb{R}^{n|m}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\left(\mathbb{R}^{0|1}\right)^{m}.

Notice that if
f
is a morphism of algebraic theories, and
A:\mathbf{T}^{\prime}\to\mathscr{C}
is a
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
-algebra in
\mathscr{C},
then the composition

\mathbf{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{T}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{C}

is a
\mathbf{T}
-algebra in
\mathscr{C},
which we denote by
f^{*}A.
This induces a functor:

f^{*}:\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\to\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right).

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an
\infty
-category in which sifted colimits commute with finite products and let
f:\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
be a morphism of algebraic theories. Then

f^{*}:\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\to\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)

preserves limits and sifted colimits.

Proof.

Notice that if
f:\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is a morphism of algebraic theories, then we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}.}

Since both
\infty
-categories are monadic over
\mathscr{C}
by Proposition
2.19
, and conservative functors reflect limits and colimits that they preserve, the result follows.
∎

Corollary 2.24
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is a presentable
\infty
-category in which sifted colimits commute with finite limits then
f^{*}
has a left adjoint

f_{!}:\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\to\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right).

When
\mathscr{C}
is the
\infty
-category of spaces
\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc},
this can be described more explicitly:
We can take the left Kan-extension along the Yoneda embedding:

\textstyle{\operatorname{PShv}\left(\mathbf{T}^{op}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Lan}_{y_{\mathbf{T}}}\left(y_{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}\circ f^{op}\right)}
\textstyle{\operatorname{PShv}\left(\mathbf{T}^{\prime op}\right)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}^{op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y_{\mathbf{T}}}
\scriptstyle{f^{op}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{T}^{\prime op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{y_{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}}

This is the unique colimit preserving functor which agrees with
f
on representables. Corollary
2.16
then implies that
\operatorname{Lan}_{y_{\mathbf{T}}}\left(y_{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}\circ f^{op}\right)
restricts to a functor between the respective categories of algebras. For example, if
f
is a morphism of Lawvere theories,
f_{!}
can then be characterized as the unique colimit preserving functor sending each finitely generated free algebra
\mathbf{T}\left\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots x_{n}\right\}
to
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}\left\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots x_{n}\right\}.
Since every algebra is a sifted colimit of finitely generated free ones, this uniquely determines the functor
f_{!}.

Corollary 2.25
.

If
f:\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
is a morphism of algebraic theories which has a right adjoint
g,
then functor

f_{!}:\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)\to\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)

has both a left and right adjoint, and in particular preserves small limits and colimits.

Proof.

Since
f
is a morphism of algebraic theories,
f_{!}
has
f^{*}
as a right adjoint. Since
g
is a right adjoint, it preserves limits, and hence is an morphism of algebraic theories. Consequently, we have two induced adjunctions

\textstyle{\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{!}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}}

and

\textstyle{\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{!}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\ast}}

Using the inclusion
\mathbf{Alg}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{PShv}\left(\mathbf{T}^{op}\right),
and similarly for
\mathbf{T}^{\prime}
, we can conclude that
f_{!}\simeq g^{*}.
Indeed, let
t\in\mathbf{T}
and
s\in\mathbf{T}^{\prime}.
Then we have the following string of natural equivalences:

\displaystyle g^{*}\left(j\left(t\right)\right)\left(s\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(t,g\left(s\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\mathbf{T}^{\prime}}\left(f\left(t\right),s\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle j\left(f\left(t\right)\right)\left(s\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle f_{!}\left(j\left(t\right)\right)\left(s\right).

Finally, we are done since
g^{*}
has
g_{!}
as a left adjoint.
∎

2.2.
Finitely presented algebras

Definition 2.26
.

(1)

A
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
X\in\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)
is
finitely generated
if the functor

\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}

corepresented by
X
preserves small filtered colimits consisting only of monomorphisms. Denote the full subcategory on the finitely generated algebras by
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{\mathbf{fg}}
.

(2)

A
\mathbf{T}
-algebra
X\in\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)
is
finitely presented
if the functor

\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{\mathbf{fp}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}

corepresented by
X
preserves small filtered colimits; that is, if
X
is a compact object. Denote the full subcategory on the finitely presented algebras by
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{\mathbf{fp}}
.

Lemma 2.27
.

[
11
, Lemma 3.21]

The subcategory
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{\mathbf{fp}}
is the smallest subcategory of
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)
containing the finitely generated free algebras and closed under finite colimits and retracts. Moreover, any finitely presented algebra is a retract of a finite colimit of finitely generated free algebras.

Theorem 2.28
.

[
11
, Theorem 3.22]

Let
\mathscr{C}
be any idempotent complete
\infty
-category with finite limits. Then composition with
j^{\mathbf{fp}}
induces an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\operatorname{Fun}^{\mathsf{lex}}\left(\left(\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)^{\mathbf{fp}}\right)^{op},\mathscr{C}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Fun}^{\Pi\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu}\left(\mathbf{T},\mathscr{C}\right)=\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)

whose inverse is given by right Kan extension.

2.3.
Homotopical algebras

When
\mathbf{T}
is a
1
-category, then the projective model structure on
\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathbf{T},\mathrm{Set}^{\Delta^{op}}\right)
restricts to the subcategory of simplicial
\mathbf{T}
-algebras

\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathrm{Set}\right)^{\Delta^{op}}\cong\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathrm{Set}^{\Delta^{op}}\right).

We have the following result, originally due to Bergner:

Theorem 2.29
.

[
4
, Theorem 5.1]
,
[
22
, Corollary 5.5.9.2]

There is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

N_{hc}\left(\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathrm{Set}\right)^{\Delta^{op}}_{proj.}\right)\simeq\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)

between the homotopy coherent nerve of the category of simplicial
\mathbf{T}
-algebras, endowed with the projective model structure, and the
\infty
-category of
\mathbf{T}
-algebras in
\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}.

Example 2.30
.

By the above,
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}_{\mathrm{Ab}}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of simplicial abelian groups, which by the Dold-Kan correspondence is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of non-positively graded cochain complexes of abelian groups
\mathbf{Ch}^{\leq 0}\left(\mathrm{Ab}\right).

Example 2.31
.

Similarly, by the monoidal Dold-Kan correspondence, if
k
is a field of characteristic zero,
\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{Com}_{k}}\left(\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}\right)
can be identified with the
\infty
-category
dg\mbox{-}\left(\mathbf{Com}_{k}\right)_{\leq 0}
of commutative non-positively graded differential
k
-algebras (using cohomological grading conventions).

3.
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras

Definition 3.1
.

A
classical

\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra
is a
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra in
\mathrm{Set},
where
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
is the algebraic theory of Example
2.12
. Similarly, a
classical

{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebra
is a
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebra in
\mathrm{Set},
where
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
is the
2
-sorted algebraic theory of Example
2.13
.

The prototypical example of a
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-ring is the ring of smooth functions
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(M\right)
on a smooth manifold
M.
This ring has an
n
-ary operation associated to each smooth function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(M\right)^{n}\cong\mathbb{H}\mathrm{om}_{\mathsf{Mfd}}\left(M,\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(M\right)

\displaystyle\left(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}\right)=\varphi:M\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle f\circ\varphi.

As an algebra for the algebraic theory
\mathbf{C}^{\infty},

\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(M\right)
is the functor

\mathbb{H}\mathrm{om}_{\mathsf{Mfd}}\left(M,\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right):\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\to\mathrm{Set},

and the underlying set of
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(M\right)
is recovered as the value of this functor on
\mathbb{R}.
Recall that
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
is the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Mfd}
on the manifolds of the form
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\geq 0,
and that this is an algebraic theory with a single generator
r:=\mathbb{R}.
The free
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra on
n
generators is
j\left(r^{n}\right)=\mathbb{H}\mathrm{om}_{\mathsf{Mfd}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right),
i.e.

\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}\cong\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right).

An analogous story holds for the
2
-sorted algebraic theory
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
. The prototypical example of a
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebra, also called a
super
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-ring
, is the superalgebra of smooth functions on a supermanifold
\mathcal{M}.
As a functor, this algebra is simply
\mathbb{H}\mathrm{om}_{\mathsf{SMfd}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right).
The main difference between this theory and the theory of
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras is that there are two generators— the even generator
\mathbb{R}
and the odd generator
\mathbb{R}^{0|1}.
If
\mathcal{M}
is a supermanifold, then the even elements
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)_{0}=\mathbb{H}\mathrm{om}_{\mathsf{SMfd}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}\right)
of its superalgebra of smooth functions are smooth functions to
\mathbb{R},
whereas its odd elements
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)_{1}=\mathbb{H}\mathrm{om}_{\mathsf{SMfd}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathbb{R}^{0|1}\right)
correspond to smooth functions to the odd line
\mathbb{R}^{0|1}.
In this formalism, there are no in-homogeneous elements, and so there is an underlying
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded set of
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{M}\right).
The free
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebra on
n
even and
m
odd generators is

\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{m}\}\cong\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n|m}\right)\cong\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\underset{\mathbb{R}}{\otimes}\wedge^{\bullet}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right),

where
\wedge^{\bullet}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
is the exterior algebra with its Koszul grading and the tensor product is as supercommutative
\mathbb{R}
-algebras.

One of the main reasons that
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras are reasonable to work with is the following theorem:

Theorem 3.2
.

[
25
, Proposition 1.2]
,
[
10
, Corollary 2.116]

Let
I
be an ideal of the underlying
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\sharp}
of a classical
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}.
Then
I
is a
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-congruence and
\mathcal{A}_{\sharp}/I
carries the canonical structure of a
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra making
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}_{\sharp}/I=:\mathcal{A}/I
a
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-homomorphism. Similarly, for
\mathcal{A}
a classical
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebra with underlying supercommutative
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\sharp},
and
I
a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}

homogeneous
ideal of
\mathcal{A}_{\sharp}.

The category of classical
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras is presentable, so, in particular, it has coproducts. Coproducts in the category of commutative
k
-algebras are computed by tensor product: If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
are commutative rings, their coproduct is
\mathcal{A}\underset{k}{\otimes}\mathsf{B}
— the tensor product of
k
-modules equipped with an induced
k
-algebra structure. This is no longer the case for (classical)
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras, and this is a good thing, since we have for
n,m>0,

\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\underset{\mathbb{R}}{\otimes}\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)\subsetneq\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right).

But
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)
are the free
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras on
n
and
m
generators respectively, so their coproduct must be the free
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra on
\left(n+m\right)
generators, namely
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right).
We denote the coproduct in the category of classical
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-rings by
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\displaystyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\textstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}.
Then we have

\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\displaystyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\textstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\right)\cong\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\right).

Similarly, we denote pushouts by

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{B}\underset{\mathcal{A}}{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\displaystyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\textstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}}\mathcal{C}.}

The story is completely analogous for classical
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebras, and we denote the coproduct again by
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\displaystyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\textstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}},
which is justified since the inclusion

\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathrm{Set}\right)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Alg}_{{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}}\left(\mathrm{Set}\right)

preserve coproducts.

The tensor product
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\displaystyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\textstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}
is remarkably well-behaved.

Proposition 3.3
.

[
25
, Chapter 1, Theorem 2.8]

If
f:M\to N
and
g:L\to N
are transverse maps of smooth manifolds, then

\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(M\times_{N}L\right)\cong\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(M\right)\underset{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(N\right)}{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\displaystyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\textstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}}\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(L\right).

Lemma 3.4
.

Every supermanifold is a retract of an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n|m}
for some
n
and
m.

Proof.

Let
M
be the underlying even manifold of a supermanifold
\mathcal{M}.
By Batchelor’s theorem
[
2
]
,
\mathcal{M}\cong\Pi E
for
E
a finite dimensional vector bundle over
M.

E
in turn is a direct summand of a trivial bundle
V
of rank
m
for some
m.
By the tubular neighborhood theorem combined with Whitney’s embedding theorem, write
M
as a retract of an open subset
U
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Then
\Pi V\cong M\times\mathbb{R}^{0|m}
is a retract of
U\times\mathbb{R}^{0|m}
which is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n|m}.
Since
E
is a retract of
V,

\Pi E
is a retract of
\Pi V,
and the result follows.
∎

Theorem 3.5
.

If
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
and
g:\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{N}
are transverse maps of supermanifolds, then

\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{M}\times_{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{L}\right)\cong\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{M}\right)\underset{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{N}\right)}{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\displaystyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\textstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}}\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{L}\right).

Proof.

Analyzing the proof of
[
25
, Chapter 1, Theorem 2.8]
, it consists of two separate facts. Firstly, that the above holds when
g
is a closed embedding, and secondly that it holds when
\mathcal{N}
is a point (i.e. binary products are sent to coproducts). The proof of the first fact goes through with virtually no modification in the supergoemetric setting. The proof of the second fact follows formally from the fact that every smooth manifold
M
is a retract of an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
So we are done by Lemma
3.4
.
∎

The following fact is fundamental:

Proposition 3.6
.

The canonical functors

\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right):\mathsf{Mfd}\to\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathrm{Set}\right)

\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right):\mathsf{SMfd}\to\mathbf{Alg}{{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}}\left(\mathrm{Set}\right)

are fully faithful.

We combine these results into the following theorem for reference

Theorem 3.7
.

[
11
, Theorem 4.51]

The canonical functor
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}:\mathsf{SMfd}\to\mathbf{d}{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}\mathbf{Alg}^{op}
is fully faithful and preserves transverse pullbacks.

3.0.1.
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-completion

Denote by
\tau:\mathsf{SComm}_{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
the evident morphism of algebraic theories from the theory of supercommutative
\mathbb{R}
-algebras to the theory of
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebras. By Corollary
2.24
there is an induced adjunction between their associated categories of algebras

\textstyle{\mathsf{SCAlg}_{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}\mathsf{Alg}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)_{\sharp}}

where
\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)_{\sharp}=\tau^{*}
and
\widehat{\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)}=\tau_{!}.

Definition 3.8
.

Given a supercommutative
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\mathcal{A},
its
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-completion
is
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}.
Given a (homogeneous) ideal
I
of
\mathcal{A},
denote by
\widehat{I}
the extension of
I
along

\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}\widehat{A}_{\sharp},

the unit of the adjunction
\tau_{!}\dashv\tau^{*}.

Example 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a finitely generated (classical) supercommutative
\mathbb{R}
-algebra. Then
\mathcal{A}
has a presentation as
\mathbb{R}\left[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{m}\right]/I,
for a homogeneous ideal. The
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-completion of
\mathcal{A}
is given as

\widehat{A}=\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{m|n}\right)/\widehat{I}.

The
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-completion can sometimes be destructive, as the following example illustrates:

Example 3.10
.

Consider
\mathbb{C}
as a commutative
\mathbb{R}
-algebra. Notice

\mathbb{C}\cong\mathbb{R}\left[x\right]/\left(x^{2}+1\right).

Since
\mathbb{R}\left[x\right]
is free and
\widehat{\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)}
is a left adjoint,
\widehat{\mathbb{R}\left[x\right]}=\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\{x\}=\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right).
The extension of the principal ideal
\left(x^{2}+1\right)
is still a principal ideal and generated by the same element
x^{2}+1.
But
x^{2}+1
is invertible on
\mathbb{R},
as it is never zero, so is a unit in
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right),
hence
\widehat{\left(x^{2}+1\right)}=\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\{x\},
and

\widehat{\mathbb{C}}=0.

3.0.2.
Localizations

Definition 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a classical
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebra, and let
a\in\mathcal{A}_{0}.
A morphism
\varphi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{B}
of
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-rings is a
localization of
\mathcal{A}
at
a
if
\mathsf{B}
fits into a pushout diagram

\textstyle{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{a}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{0\right\}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{B}.}

In this case we write
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}\left[a^{-1}\right].
If
S
is a multiplicatively closed subset of
\mathcal{A}_{0},
then the
localization of
\mathcal{A}
at
S
is the filtered colimit

\mathcal{A}_{S}:=\underset{a\in S}{\underrightarrow{\mbox{colim}\vskip 0.5pt}\mspace{4mu}}\mathcal{A}\left[a^{-1}\right].

The localization of
\mathcal{A}
at a prime ideal
P
is by definition the localization of
\mathcal{A}
at
\mathcal{A}\setminus P.

Lemma 3.12

(Lemma
[
14
, 5.1.13]
)
.

Any localization
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}\left[a^{-1}\right]
is flat.

Corollary 3.13
.

Any localization of a
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra is flat.

3.1.
Reduced
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras

Definition 3.14
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a classical
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra, and let
x:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
be an
\mathbb{R}
-point of
\mathcal{A}.
Let
\mathfrak{m}_{x}
be the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{x}:=\ker\left(x\right).
Denote the natural morphism to the localization by

\mathbf{germ}_{x}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}_{x}

where
\mathcal{A}_{x}
is the
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-localization of
\mathcal{A}
at
\mathfrak{m}_{x}.
Given an element
a\in\mathcal{A},
its
germ at
x
is
\mathbf{germ}_{x}\left(a\right).
Similarly, given an ideal
I
of
\mathcal{A},
its
germ at
x
,
I_{x}
, is the extension of
I
along the homomorphism
\mathbf{germ}_{x}.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
I
be an ideal of a classical
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}.
Let
x:\mathcal{A}/I\to\mathbb{R}
be an
\mathbb{R}
-point. Then

\left(\mathcal{A}/I\right)_{x}\cong\mathcal{A}_{x}/I_{x}.

Proof.

Firstly, let
\pi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}/I
and let
a\in\mathcal{A}.
Then

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{A}/I\right)\left\{\pi\left(a\right)^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{A}/I\right)\underset{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\displaystyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\textstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}}\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{0\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{A}\underset{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)}{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\displaystyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\textstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}{\mathbin{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-13.73mu\resizebox{1305270}{540744}{$\phantom{x}{}_{\phantom{.}{}^{\infty}}$}$\crcr$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$\crcr}}}}}\mathbf{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{0\right\}\right)\right)/\left(I^{e}\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\left\{a^{-1}\right\}/I^{e}

where
I^{e}
is the extension of the ideal. The result now follows since any localization is a filtered colimit of localizations by a single element.
∎

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a classical
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra and let
I
be an ideal of
\mathcal{A}.
The ideal

I^{\wedge}=\left\{a\in\mathcal{A}\mspace{3mu}|\mspace{3mu}a_{x}\in I_{x},\mspace{5mu}\forall\mspace{3mu}x:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}\right\}

is called the
germ-completion
of
I
. An ideal
I
is called
germ-determined
if
I=I^{\wedge}.
A classical
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra is called germ-determined if its zero ideal is.

Definition 3.17
.

An element
a
in a classical
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
is
locally nilpotent
if for all
x:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R},

\mathbf{germ}_{x}\left(a\right)
is nilpotent in
\mathcal{A}_{x}.
Denote by
\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
the ideal of locally nilpotent elements.

Definition 3.18
.

A classical
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra is said to be
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-reduced
if it has no non-zero locally nilpotent elements.

Remark 3.19
.

This is a different definition than
[
24
, Definition 2.1]
. What we are defining should perhaps be called
Archimidean reduced

Remark 3.20
.

Note that
\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
is germ-determined.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a finitely generated classical
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra. Then

\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)=\mathbf{Nil}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)^{\wedge},

i.e. an element
a\in\mathcal{A}
is locally nilpotent if and only if it is in the germ-completion of the nilradical.

Proof.

Note that, since
\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
is germ-determined, we can reduce to the case when
\mathcal{A}
is germ-determined. Consider the nilradical
\mathbf{Nil}\left(\mathcal{A}\right).
If it is not germ-determined, then it is not a complete-module in the sense of
[
15
, Definition 5.25]
. However, we can identify
\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
as its completion — that is global sections of
\mathbf{MSpec}\left(\mathbf{Nil}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right).
Since
\mathbf{MSpec}\left(\mathbf{Nil}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)
is quasi-coherent, for all
b\in\mathcal{A},

\mathcal{A}\left\{b^{-1}\right\}\underset{\mathcal{A}}{\otimes}\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\cong\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\left\{b^{-1}\right\}\right).

Let
U_{z}
be the open subset of
\mathbf{Spec}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
corresponding to
\mathbf{Spec}\,\left(\mathcal{A}\left\{z^{-1}\right\}\right).
It follows that for all
x:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{x}\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathbf{MSpec}\left(\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)_{x}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{z\notin\mathfrak{m}_{x}}{\underrightarrow{\mbox{colim}\vskip 0.5pt}\mspace{4mu}}\mathbf{MSpec}\left(\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\left(U_{z}\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{z\notin\mathfrak{m}_{x}}{\underrightarrow{\mbox{colim}\vskip 0.5pt}\mspace{4mu}}\mathcal{A}\left\{b^{-1}\right\}\underset{\mathcal{A}}{\otimes}\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\left(\underset{z\notin\mathfrak{m}_{x}}{\underrightarrow{\mbox{colim}\vskip 0.5pt}\mspace{4mu}}\mathcal{A}\left\{b^{-1}\right\}\right)\underset{\mathcal{A}}{\otimes}\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{x}\underset{\mathcal{A}}{\otimes}\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right).

Now, suppose that
a\in\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right).
Then it follows that
\mathbf{germ}_{x}\left(a\right)\in\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)_{x}
for all
x:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R},
and hence in
\mathbf{Nil}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)^{\wedge}.
Conversely, suppose that
a\in\mathbf{Nil}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)^{\wedge}.
Then, it also follows that for all
x,

\mathbf{germ}_{x}\left(a\right)\in\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{x}\right),
and hence
a
is locally nilpotent.
∎

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
be
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras, with
\mathsf{B}

\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-reduced. Then for any map

\varphi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathsf{B},

\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\subseteq\ker\left(\varphi\right).

Proof.

Let
a\in\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right).
Let
x:\mathsf{B}\to\mathbb{R}.
Consider the induced map

\varphi_{x}:\mathcal{A}_{x\circ\varphi}\to\mathsf{B}_{x}.

Then
\varphi_{x}\left(\mathbf{germ}_{x}\left(a\right)\right)
is nilpotent in
\mathsf{B}_{x}.
But

\varphi_{x}\left(\mathbf{germ}_{x}\left(a\right)\right)=\varphi\left(a\right)_{x}.

So
\varphi\left(a\right)
is locally nilpotent in
\mathsf{B},
and hence zero.
∎

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a finitely generated
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra. Then
\mathcal{A}/\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
is
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-reduced.

Proof.

Let
\pi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}/\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right),
and let
\left[a\right]
be a locally nilpotent element of
\mathcal{A}/\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right),
with
a\in\mathcal{A}.
By Lemma
3.22
, it follows that for any
x:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R},
there is a factorization
x=y\circ\pi,
for
y:\mathcal{A}/\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\to\mathbb{R}.
Let
y
be such a
\mathbb{R}
-point. Then
\mathbf{germ}_{y}\left(\left[a\right]\right)
is nilpotent, so there exists an integer
n_{y}
such that
\mathbf{germ}_{y}\left(\left[a\right]\right)^{n_{y}}=0
in
\left(\mathcal{A}/\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)_{y}.
So
\mathbf{germ}_{x}\left(a^{n_{y}}\right)
is in the kernel of

\pi_{x}:\mathcal{A}_{x}\to\left(\mathcal{A}/\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\right)_{y}

which is
\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)_{x}
by Lemma
3.15
. But this is the same as
\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{x}\right)
by the proof of Lemma
3.21
. Hence
\mathbf{germ}_{x}\left(a^{n_{y}}\right)
is nilpotent in
\mathcal{A}_{x}.
Since
x
was arbitrary, we conclude that
a\in\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right),
and hence
\left[a\right]=0.

∎

Definition 3.24
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a classical
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebra. Its
reduction
is
\mathcal{A}_{red}:=\mathcal{A}/\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right).
If
\mathcal{A}
is a classical
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-algebra, then its reduction is defined by
\mathcal{A}_{red}:=\left(\mathcal{A}_{0}\right)_{red}.

Corollary 3.25
.

The category of reduced finitely generated
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras is reflective in finitely generated
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras, with the left adjoint being

\mathcal{A}\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{red}=\mathcal{A}/\mathbf{Nil}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right).

4.
Derived manifolds

4.1.
A universal property

Morally, derived manifolds are objects that arise by iteratively taking derived fibered products and retracts starting with ordinary manifolds and smooth maps. There have been many proposed models for what a derived manifold ought to be
[
33
,
16
,
6
,
9
]
. The first was given by Spivak
[
33
]
, and Joyce has a very well-developed theory of
d-manifolds
, with important applications to symplectic topology. A common thread of all of these approaches is the prominent role played by
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras. This is not a coincidence, as it turns out that derived manifolds and
\mathbf{C}^{\infty}
-algebras are intimately linked at the categorical level.

Whatever the
\infty
-category
\mathsf{DMfd}
is, it should receive a fully faithful functor

i:\mathsf{Mfd}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{DMfd}

and
i
should preserve transverse pullbacks and the terminal object. Moreover,
\mathsf{DMfd}
should be closed under taking fibered products. One way to phrase this is to ask for
\mathsf{DMfd}
to have finite limits. Finally, one would make sense of the idea that derived manifolds are completely determined by how they are built out of manifolds using fibered products. This leads to the following definition:

Definition 4.1
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathsf{DMfd}
of derived manifolds is the (essentially unique)
\infty
-category with finite limits, together with a functor
i:\mathsf{Mfd}\to\mathsf{DMfd}
which preserves transverse pullbacks and the terminal object, such that for all
\infty
-categories
\mathscr{C}
with finite limits, composition along
i
induces an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\operatorname{Fun}^{\mathbf{lex}}\left(\mathsf{DMfd},\mathscr{C}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Fun}^{\pitchfork}\left(\mathsf{Mfd},\mathscr{C}\right)

between the
\infty
-category of finite limit preserving functors from
\mathsf{DMfd}
to
\mathscr{C}
and the
\infty
