C\in\mathscr{C}.
It follows that the Yoneda embedding induces a fully faithful inclusion

\mathbf{Alg}_{T}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Alg}_{T_{!}}\left(\Psh\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\right).

Observe that since
F_{\mathbf{Kl}}
is essentially surjective, it follows immediately that
\left(F_{\mathbf{Kl}}\right)^{*}
is conservative, and since it has a right adjoint
\left(F_{\mathbf{Kl}}\right)_{*}
it preserves all colimits. It follows from Theorem
13.1
, that the adjunction

\left(F_{\mathbf{Kl}}\right)_{!}\dashv\left(F_{\mathbf{Kl}}\right)^{*}\simeq\left(U_{\mathbf{Kl}}\right)_{!},

is monadic, and hence there is a canonically induced equivalence of
\infty
-categories

k:\Psh\left(\mathbf{Kl}\left(T\right)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{Alg}_{T_{!}}\left(\Psh\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\right).

Using the above equivalence
k,
we can identify the essential image of
\mathbf{Alg}_{T}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)
under the fully faithful inclusion into
\mathbf{Alg}_{T_{!}}\left(\Psh\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\right)
with the full subcategory of
\Psh\left(\mathbf{Kl}\left(T\right)\right)
on those presheaves
X
such that
\left(F_{\mathbf{Kl}}\right)^{*}
is representable. The result now follows.
∎

Lemma 13.4
.

Let
f:E\to F
be an epimorphism in an
\infty
-topos
\mathcal{E}
. Then

f^{*}:\mathcal{E}/F\to\mathcal{E}/E

is conservative.

Proof.

Suppose that
a:A\to Y

b:B\to Y
and
h:a\to b
in
\mathcal{E}/Y
are such that
f^{*}h
is an equivalence. Consider the following commutative diagram in which each square is a pullback square

\textstyle{f^{\ast}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f^{\ast}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underset{\sim}{f^{\ast}h}}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y.}

The double headed arrows are effective epimorphisms. The result now follows from
[
22
, Lemma 6.2.3.16]
.
∎

Theorem 13.5
.

Let
f:E\to F
be an epimorphism in an
\infty
-topos
\mathcal{E}.
Let
M
be the monad
f_{*}f^{*}
and let
C
be the comonad
f^{*}f_{!},
both of which are on
\mathcal{E}/E
. Then there are canonical equivalences

\mathbf{CoAlg}_{C}\left(\mathcal{E}/E\right)\simeq\mathcal{E}/F\simeq\mathbf{Alg}_{M}\left(\mathcal{E}/E\right)

between the Eilenberg-Moore category of coalgebras for
C,
and the slice
\infty
-topos
\mathcal{E}/F,
and the Eilenberg-Moore category of algebras for
M.
More precisely, the adjunction
f_{!}\dashv f^{*}
is monadic and
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
is comonadic.

Proof.

By the dual to
[
20
, Theorem 4.7.3.5]
, to show that the adjunction

\textstyle{\mathcal{E}/E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}/F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}

is comonadic, it suffices to show that certain types of limits are preserved by
f^{*}
— but here there is nothing to show since
f^{*}
is right adjoint to
f_{!},
and
f^{*}
is conservative— which is the content of Lemma
13.4
. The dual argument is analogous: we need that
f^{*}
is conservative— which we just proved— and we need
f^{*}
to preserve certain colimits, but it preserves all of them since it is a left adjoint.
∎

Corollary 13.6
.

If
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}
is formally smooth, then there are canonical equivalences of
\infty
-categories

\mathbf{Alg}_{\mathbf{T}^{\infty}_{M}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}/E\right)\simeq\mathbf{CoAlg}_{\mathcal{J}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{X}}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}/\mathcal{X}\right)\simeq\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}/\mathcal{X}_{dR}

Lemma 13.7
.

Let
f:E\to F
be an epimorphism in an
\infty
-topos
\mathcal{E}
. Then the induced functor

f^{\star}:\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/F\right)\to\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/E\right)

is conservative.

Proof.

Informally, objects of the tangent
\infty
-topos consist of pairs
\left(E,\mathcal{G}\right)
of an object
E\in\mathcal{E},
and
\mathcal{G}\in\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/E\right),
and morphisms

\left(E,\mathcal{G}\right)\to\left(F,\mathcal{F}\right)

consists of pairs
\left(f,\alpha\right)
where

g:E\to F

and

\alpha:g^{*}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}.

Consider the functor

\zeta:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{T}\mathcal{E}

which sends an object
E
to
\left(E,0\right).
It is left adjoint to the forgetful functor

q:\mathcal{T}\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}.

by the initiality of the zero spectrum. Notice that
\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/E\right)
can be identified with the fiber

\textstyle{\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/E\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}\mathcal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\ast\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}.}

Observe that a morphism in
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{E}
corresponding to a pair
\left(g,\alpha\right)
is an equivalence if and only if both
g
and
\alpha
are. It follows that the above canonical functor

\displaystyle\mu_{E}:\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/E\right)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\left(E,\mathcal{G}\right)

is conservative. Let
\mathcal{F}
be an object in the stabilization of
\mathcal{E}/F.
Then we have a canonical pullback diagram in
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{E}

\textstyle{\mu_{E}\left(\eta^{\star}\mathcal{F}\right)=\left(E,\eta^{\star}\mathcal{F}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(F,\mathcal{F}\right)=\mu_{F}\left(\mathcal{F}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left(E,0\right)=\mu_{E}\left(0\right)=\zeta\left(E\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta\left(f\right)}
\textstyle{\zeta\left(F\right)=\left(F,0\right)=\mu_{F}\left(0\right).}

In other words,

\mu_{E}\left(f^{\star}\mathcal{F}\right)=\zeta\left(f\right)^{*}\mu_{F}\left(\mathcal{F}\right),

where

\zeta\left(f\right)^{*}:\mathcal{T}\mathcal{E}/\zeta\left(F\right)\to\mathcal{T}\mathcal{E}/\zeta\left(E\right).

Since both
\mu_{E}
and
\mu_{F}
are conservative, it suffices to show that
\zeta\left(f\right)^{*}
is conservative. However, since
\zeta
is a left adjoint, it preserves epimorphisms, and hence
\zeta\left(f\right)
is an epimorphism, and we are done by Lemma
13.4
.
∎

Lemma 13.8
.

Let
F:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F}
be an essential geometric morphism of
\infty
-topoi, i.e. the inverse image functor
F^{*}
has a left adjoint
F_{!}.
Then for any presentable
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C},
there is a triple of adjunctions

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 23.34727pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.34727pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Shv}\left(\mathcal{E},\mathscr{C}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.74828pt\raise 9.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{!}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.34727pt\raise 9.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.34727pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.91495pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f^{\ast}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.91495pt\raise-9.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{\ast}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.34727pt\raise-9.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.34727pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 77.34727pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\;\operatorname{Shv}\left(\mathcal{F},\mathscr{C}\right)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Proof.

Notice that
\operatorname{Shv}\left(\mathcal{E},\mathscr{C}\right)
is just the tensor product of
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathscr{C}
in the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathfrak{Pr}^{L}
of presentable
\infty
-categories and left adjoint functors. The inverse image functor

F^{*}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{E}

is an arrow in this category, so define

f^{*}:=F^{*}\otimes\mathscr{C}.

This functor is a left adjoint, so denote its right adjoint by
f_{*}.
The functor
F_{!}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F}
is also an arrow in
\mathfrak{Pr}^{L}.
Denote by

f_{!}:=F_{!}\otimes\mathscr{C}.

This functor has a right adjoint, and to finish proving the lemma, we need to show it can be identified with
f^{*}.
Consider the canonical functor

\operatorname{Fun}^{L}\left(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}\right)\to\operatorname{Fun}^{L}\left(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathscr{C},\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathscr{C}\right)

induced by

\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)\otimes\mathscr{C}:\mathfrak{Pr}^{L}\to\mathfrak{Pr}^{L},

and consider the analogous functor

\operatorname{Fun}^{L}\left(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}\right)\to\operatorname{Fun}^{L}\left(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathscr{C},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathscr{C}\right).

The diagrams expressing the triangle equations for the unit
\eta
and counit
\varepsilon
for the adjunction
F_{!}\dashv F^{*}
are commutative diagrams in
\operatorname{Fun}^{L}\left(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}\right)
and
\operatorname{Fun}^{L}\left(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}\right)
. Their images in
\operatorname{Fun}^{L}\left(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathscr{C},\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathscr{C}\right)
and
\operatorname{Fun}^{L}\left(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathscr{C},\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathscr{C}\right)
respectively are the triangle equations expressing
\eta\otimes\mathscr{C}
and
\varepsilon\otimes\mathscr{C}
as the unit and co-unit for an adjunction between
f_{!}
and
f^{*}.

∎

Corollary 13.9
.

If
f:E\to F
is an epimorphism in an
\infty
-topos
\mathcal{E}
, the adjunction

\textstyle{\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/E\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{!}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/F\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\star}}

is monadic, and the adjunction

\textstyle{\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/E\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\star}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/F\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\star}}

is comonadic.

Proof.

Note that for an
\infty
-topos
\mathcal{F},
there are canonical equivalences

\operatorname{Shv}\left(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pt}\right)\simeq\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pt}\simeq\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{F}\right).

By Lemma
13.7
,
f^{\star}
is conservative, and as it has both a left and a right adjoint, the result follows from Theorem
13.1
.
∎

Corollary 13.10
.

Let
\left(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O}\right)
be a
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-ringed
\infty
-topos. Suppose that
f:E\to F
in
\mathcal{E}
is an epimorphism. Then the adjunction

\textstyle{\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\star}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\star}}

is comonadic.

Proof.

Notice that
f^{\star}\mathcal{O}_{F}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{E},
so that
f^{\star}\mathcal{F}\simeq f^{*}\mathcal{F}
for all
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-modules
\mathcal{F}.
It follows that this
f^{\star}
is the restriction of the one between stabilizations. Observe that

\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}\simeq\mathbf{Mod}_{H\mathcal{O}_{E}}\left(\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/E\right)\right),

hence
\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}
is monadic over
\mathbf{Stab}\left(\mathcal{E}/E\right)
via

H\mathcal{O}_{E}\wedge\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)\dashv U,

where
U
is the forgetful functor. It follows that
U
is conservative. The same is true for
F.
Hence
f^{\star}
is conservative. It also preserves limits, since it does on stabilizations and
U
reflects limits. We are now done by Theorem
13.1
.
∎

Lemma 13.11
.

Let
f:E\to F
be an epimorphism in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}
. Then if for an
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-module
\mathcal{F},

f^{\star}\mathcal{F}
is a quasi-coherent sheaf over
E,
then
\mathcal{F}
is quasi-coherent over
F.

Proof.

Notice that by Lemma
9.62
, we can reduce to the case when
F\in\mathscr{D}.
In this case, since
f
is an epimorphism, there is a cover
\left(U_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow F\right)_{\alpha}
by affine open
{\mathbf{SC}^{\infty}}
-subschemes over which
f
admits local sections
\lambda_{\alpha}.
It follows that for all
\alpha,

\mathcal{F}|_{U_{\alpha}}\simeq\lambda_{\alpha}^{\star}f^{\star}\mathcal{F},

and hence
\mathcal{F}|_{U_{\alpha}}
is quasi-coherent for all
\alpha.
Let
M:=\Gamma_{F}\left(\mathcal{F}\right)\in\widehat{\mathbf{Mod}}_{\mathcal{A}},
for
F=\mathbf{Spec}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}\right).
We want to show that the canonical map

\epsilon:\rm{MSpec}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}/F}\left(M\right)\to\mathcal{F}

is an equivalence. Since

\pi:\underset{\alpha}{\coprod}U_{\alpha}\to\mathcal{F}

is an epimorphism, it follows from Corollary
13.10
that
\pi^{\star}
is conservative, so it suffices to check that
\pi^{\star}\left(\epsilon\right)
is an equivalence. By hypothesis, letting
M_{\alpha}:=\mathcal{F}\left(U_{\alpha}\right)\in\widehat{\mathbf{Mod}}_{\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}},
where
U_{\alpha}=\mathbf{Spec}_{\mathbf{C}^{\infty}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}\right)
for each
\alpha,
we have that the canonical maps

\rm{MSpec}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}/U_{\alpha}}\left(M_{\alpha}\right)\to\mathcal{F}|_{U_{\alpha}}

are equivalences. But since the restriction of
\mathcal{F}
to the topos of sheaves over the underlying space of
F
is an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{A}}
-modules sheaf, and all such module sheaves are quasi-coherent, it follows that for all
\alpha,

\mathcal{F}\left(U_{\alpha}\right)\simeq\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}\underset{\mathcal{A}}{\widehat{\otimes}}M

and thus

\rm{MSpec}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}/U_{\alpha}}\left(M_{\alpha}\right)\simeq\rm{MSpec}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}/F}\left(M\right)|_{U_{\alpha}},

and we are done.
∎

Corollary 13.12
.

Let
f:E\to F
be an epimorphism in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathfrak{Der}}
. Then the following diagram of
\infty
-categories is a pullback:

\textstyle{\mathbf{QC}\left(F\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda^{\star}\left(F\right)}
\scriptstyle{f^{\star}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\star}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{QC}\left(E\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda^{\star}\left(E\right)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Mod}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}}

Let
L:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be a functor between
\infty
-categories associated to a coCartesian fibration

p:\mathcal{M}\to\Delta^{1},

with
p^{-1}\left(0\right)=\mathscr{C}
and
p^{-1}\left(1\right)=\mathscr{D}.
By the universal property of pullbacks, there is a canonical isomorphism of simplicial sets,

\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{M}\right)\cong\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}\right),

and similarly

\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathcal{M}\right)\cong\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right).

Denote by

i_{0}:\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}

and

i_{1}:\mathscr{D}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}

the canonical inclusions. Then restriction along these functors determine canonical maps

i^{*}_{0}\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)\to\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathfrak{M}\right)

and

i_{1}^{*}:\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)\to\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathfrak{M}\right).

Lemma 13.13
.

In the situation above, suppose that
p
is also a Cartesian fibration, exhibiting a right adjoint
R
of
L.
Then
i_{1}^{*}
has a fully faithful right adjoint.

Proof.

If we take
\mathcal{M}
to explicitly be relative nerve (
[
22
, Definition 3.2.5.2]
) of the functor

\Delta^{1}\to\mathrm{Set}^{\Delta^{op}}

whose image is

L:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D},

then it follows by direct inspection that the canonical dotted map
\Psi
below is a trivial Kan fibration:

\textstyle{\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)^{\Delta^{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle ev_{0},ev_{1}\rangle}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}\right)\times\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L_{\ast}\times L^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)\times\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right),}

where
\mathcal{P}
is the pullback in simplicial sets. Informally, an object of
\mathcal{P}
is a diagram of the form

\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}.}

Notice that the right most arrow in the pullback diagram defining
\mathcal{P}
is a categorical fibration, hence the diagram is also a homotopy pullback in the Joyal model structure. In particular, this is a pullback in the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories. It follows that mapping spaces may be computed as pullbacks (in the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{S}\mskip-3.0mu\operatorname{pc}
)

\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\mathcal{P}}\left(\left(F,G,\alpha\right),\left(F^{\prime},G^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)^{\Delta^{1}}}\left(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}\right)}\left(F,F^{\prime}\right)\times\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(G,G^{\prime}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(LF,LF^{\prime}\right)\times\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(GL,GL^{\prime}\right).}

Moreover, we have a pullback diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)^{\Delta^{1}}}\left(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(GL,G^{\prime}L\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(LF,LF^{\prime}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}_{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(LF,G^{\prime}L\right).}

It follows that we have a pullback diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\mathcal{P}}\left(\left(F,G,\alpha\right),\left(F^{\prime},G^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(G,G^{\prime}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\ast}\circ L}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}\right)}\left(F,F^{\prime}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}_{\ast}\circ L}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(LF,G^{\prime}L\right).}

Now suppose
G:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{D}
is a fixed functor. Then there is a canonical diagram

\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{RGL}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{GL}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D},}

where
\varepsilon:LR\Rightarrow id_{\mathscr{D}}
is the co-unit of the adjunction. This defines a functor

\displaystyle Q_{1}:\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\left(RGL,G,\varepsilon_{GL}\right).

We will show that
Q_{1}
is right adjoint to functor

\displaystyle pr_{1}:\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)

\displaystyle\left(F,G,\alpha\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle G.

Since
pr_{1}\circ\Psi=i_{1}^{*},
this will prove that
i_{1}^{*}
has a right adjoint. Notice that since
\varepsilon
is the co-unit of the adjunction
L\dashv R,
for any

G:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{D}

and

F^{\prime}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C},

the composite

\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}\right)}\left(F^{\prime},RGL\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(LF^{\prime},LRGL\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\epsilon_{GL}\right)_{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(LF^{\prime},GL\right),}

is a equivalence. It follows that for any
\left(F^{\prime},G^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\right)
in
\mathcal{P},
since we have a pullback diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\mathcal{P}}\left(\left(F^{\prime},G^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\right),\left(RGL,G,\varepsilon_{GL}\right)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(G^{\prime},G\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\ast}\circ L}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}\right)}\left(F^{\prime},RGL\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\varepsilon_{GL}\right)_{\ast}\circ L}
\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(LF^{\prime},GL\right),}

the map

\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\mathcal{P}}\left(\left(F^{\prime},G^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\right),\left(RGL,G,\varepsilon_{GL}\right)\right)\longrightarrow\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(G^{\prime},G\right)

is an equivalence. Thus
Q_{1}
is right adjoint to
i_{1}^{*}

Notice moreover that for any endofunctor
G
on
\mathscr{D},

\displaystyle i_{1}^{*}\circ Q_{1}\left(G\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle i_{1}^{*}\left(RGL,G,\varepsilon_{GL}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle G.

Hence the co-unit of the adjunction
i_{1}^{*}\dashv Q_{1}
is an equivalence, and we deduce that
Q_{1}
is fully faithful.
∎

Proposition 13.14
.

Let
L\dashv R
be an adjunction, with
L:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
a functor between
\infty
-categories. Then the functor

\displaystyle\Xi:\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}\right)

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle R\circ G\circ L

is lax monoidal with respect to the composition monoidal structures.

Proof.

If
p:\mathcal{M}\to\Delta^{1}
is a biCartesian fibration exhibiting the adjunction, the functor

i^{*}_{0}:\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)\to\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathcal{M}\right)\cong\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}\right)

is a strict map of simplicial monoids, and thus a monoidal functor between the associated monoidal
\infty
-categories. Similarly for
i_{1}^{*}.
By Lemma
13.13
,
i_{1}^{*}
has a right adjoint
\tilde{Q}_{1}.
Under the equivalence
\Psi
from the proof of Lemma
13.13
, the composite of
i_{0}^{*}\tilde{Q}_{1}
can be identified with
pr_{0}\circ Q_{1},
where
Q_{1}
is the explicit right adjoint to
pr_{1}
given by

Q_{1}\left(G\right)=\left(LGR,G,\varepsilon_{GL}\right).

Thus, the functor
\Xi
can be identified with
i_{0}^{*}\tilde{Q}_{1}.
Since
\tilde{Q}_{1}
is right adjoint to the monoidal functor
i_{1}^{*},
it is lax monoidal. Since
i_{0}^{*}
is monoidal, it follows that
\Xi
is also lax monoidal.
∎

Lemma 13.15
.

Let
L\dashv R
exhibit
\mathscr{C}
as a coreflective subcategory of
\mathscr{D}.
Denote the co-unit of this adjunction by
\varepsilon.
Suppose that
J
is an endofunctor of
\mathscr{D}
such that for all
n,

RJ\left(\varepsilon_{J^{n}LX}\right)
is an equivalence. Denote by
\operatorname{Fun}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)
the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)
on
J^{n},
for all
n\geq 0,
(with
J^{0}=id_{\mathscr{D}}
). Then the composite

\operatorname{Fun}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Xi}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}\right)

is monoidal.

Proof.

The unit of the adjunction
L\dashv R
is what is responsible for the non-strictness of the unit identities of the lax monoidal functor
\Xi.
Since
L
is fully faithful, the unit of the adjunction
L\dashv R
is an equivalence. Hence, to show that
\Xi|_{\operatorname{Fun}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)}
is monoidal, it suffices to prove that all the comparison maps

\mu_{n}:\Xi\left(J\right)\circ\Xi\left(J\right)\circ\ldots\circ\Xi\left(J\right)\to\Xi\left(J\circ J\circ\ldots\circ J\right)

are equivalences. These maps are induced by the co-unit, and we have equivalences

\mu_{n+1}\simeq RJ\left(\varepsilon_{J^{n}LX}\right)\circ RJL\left(\mu_{n}\right)

for all
n\geq 0.
Moreover,
\mu_{0}
is the unit, which is an equivalence.
∎

Remark 13.16
.

By essentially the same proof, since the monoidal structure on

\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)\simeq\left[\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}\right)\times\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)\right]\times_{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)\times\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)}\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\right)^{\Delta^{1}}

is given ‘‘pointwise,’’ i.e.

\left(F^{\prime},G^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\right)\otimes\left(G,G,\alpha\right)=\left(F\circ F^{\prime},G\circ G^{\prime},G^{\prime}\left(\alpha\right)\circ\alpha^{\prime}_{F}\right),

under the same assumptions as Lemma
13.15
, the composite

\operatorname{Fun}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q_{1}}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)

is also monoidal.

Lemma 13.17
.

Let
L\dashv R
exhibit
\mathscr{C}
as a coreflective subcategory of
\mathscr{D}.
Denote the co-unit of this adjunction by
\varepsilon.
Let
J
be a comonad on
\mathscr{D}
such that for all
n,

RJ\left(\varepsilon_{J^{n}LX}\right)
is an equivalence. Denote by
C=LR
the comonad induced by the adjunction. Then
CJC
has a canonical structure of a comonad and there is a commutative diagram of comonads (monoid objects in the opposite category of
\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)
)

\textstyle{CJC\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{id_{\mathscr{D}}.}

Proof.

By Lemma
13.13
,
Q_{1}
is fully faithful, so by Remark
13.16
, it follows that the composite

\operatorname{Fun}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Q_{1}}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)

is a fully faithful monoidal functor. Thus,
Q_{1}\left(J\right)=\left(RJL,J,\varepsilon_{JL}\right)
is a comonoid object of
\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right).
Properly dualizing Proposition
13.14
,
i_{0}^{*}
has a
left
adjoint
Q_{0}
given by

Q_{0}\left(F\right)=\left(F,LFR,LF\left(\eta\right)\right).

(Note moreover,
\eta
is an equivalence). Hence there is a comonad
Q_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}
on
\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right),
and since
i_{0}^{*}
is monoidal (as a strict map of simplicial monoids),
Q_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}
is oplax monoidal. It follows that the canonical (essentially unique) map of comonoids

\left(RJL,J,\varepsilon_{JL}\right)\to\left(id_{\mathscr{C}},\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{D}},LF\left(\eta\right)\right)=1\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu1

induces a map of comonoids

Q_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}\left(RJL,J,\varepsilon_{JL}\right)\to Q_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}\left(id_{\mathscr{C}},\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{D}},LF\left(\eta\right)\right).

Notice

\displaystyle Q_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}\left(RJL,J,\varepsilon_{JL}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle Q_{0}\left(RJL\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\left(RJL,LRJLR,LF\left(\eta\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(RJL,CJC,LF\left(\eta\right)\right)

and

\displaystyle Q_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}\left(id_{\mathscr{C}},\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{D}},L\left(\eta\right)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle Q_{0}\left(id_{\mathscr{C}}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathscr{C}},LR,L\left(\eta\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(id_{\mathscr{C}},C,L\left(\eta\right)\right).

So there is a canonical map of comonoid objects

\left(RJL,CJC,LF\left(\eta\right)\right)\to\left(id_{\mathscr{C}},C,L\left(\eta\right)\right).

Since
i_{1}^{*}
is monoidal, we get a canonical map of comonads

CJC\to C
as desired.

Since the comonad
Q_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}
is the composition of two oplax monoidal functors, it becomes a comonoid object in the monoidal
\infty
-category

\operatorname{Fun}^{\mathsf{lax}}\left(\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)^{\otimes},\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)^{\otimes}\right).

It follows that the co-unit of
Q_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}
is a map of oplax monoidal functors

Q_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}\to id_{\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)^{\otimes}}.

Therefore, the co-unit evaluated at
\Xi\left(J\right)
gives a map of comonoids

Q_{0}\circ i_{0}^{*}\Xi\left(J\right)=\left(RJL,CJC,LF\left(\eta\right)\right)\to\left(RJL,J,\varepsilon_{JL}\right)=\Xi\left(J\right).

Applying the monoidal functor
i_{1}^{*},
this induces a map of comonads
CJC\to J.
Finally, the diagram commutes since
id_{\mathscr{D}}
is the terminal comonad on
\mathscr{D}.

∎

Remark 13.18
.

Let
p:\mathcal{M}\to\Delta^{1}
be a biCartesian fibration exhibiting an adjunction
L\dashv R
with

L:\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}.

We can identify
\mathcal{M}
with the (contravariant) relative nerve (
[
22
, Definition 3.2.5.2]
) of the functor

R:\left(\Delta^{1}\right)^{op}\to\mathrm{Set}^{\Delta^{op}}

whose image is

R:\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{C}.

The commutative diagram of simplicial sets

\textstyle{\mathscr{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id_{\mathscr{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}}

is a natural transformation

\mu:R\Rightarrow\Delta_{\mathscr{C}}.

Since the relative nerve construction if functorial, it induces a morphism

N_{\mu}\left(\Delta^{1}\right):\mathcal{M}=N_{R}\left(\Delta^{1}\right)\to N_{\Delta_{\mathscr{C}}}\left(\Delta^{1}\right)=\Delta\times\mathscr{C}

in
\mathrm{Set}^{\Delta^{op}}/\Delta^{1}.
Composition with the projection to
\mathscr{C}
yields a functor

\tau:\mathcal{M}\to\mathscr{C}

such that
\tau\circ i_{0}=id_{\mathscr{C}}.
Informally, it is given by

\tau\left(0,C\right)=C

and

\tau\left(1,D\right)=R\left(D\right).

It is convenient to recall the framework of homotopy coherent monads of
[
27
]
. We will adapt the set up for comonads. Let
\underline{\mathsf{Adj}}
denote the
2
-category with a generic adjunction. It has two objects
+
and
-.
Moreover,

\textstyle{\underline{\mathsf{Adj}}\left(+,+\right)=\Delta_{+}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathsf{Adj}}\left(-,-\right)=\Delta_{+}^{op}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathsf{Adj}}\left(-,+\right)=\Delta_{\infty}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathsf{Adj}}\left(+,-\right)=\Delta_{\infty}^{op},}

where
\Delta_{\infty}
is the full wide subcategory of
\Delta
on those morphisms which preserves top elements. Denote by
\underline{\mathsf{CMnd}}
the full subcategory on the object
-.
This is the same as the
2
-category with one object corresponding to the monoidal structure on
\Delta_{+}^{op}
given by the join construction. Regard this strict
2
-category as a category enriched in simplicial sets, via the nerve construction. Denote by
\underline{\mathrm{Set}}^{\Delta^{op}}
the category simplicial sets equipped with its canonical self-enrichment given by the fact that it is Cartesian closed. Furthermore, denote by
\underline{\mathsf{QCat}}
the full subcategory on the quasicategories. Composition in the
2
-category
\underline{\mathsf{Adj}}
equips
\Delta_{\infty}^{op}
with the canonical structure of a left module for the monoidal category
\Delta_{+}^{op},
witnessed by a canonical
2
-functor

\mathbb{W}:\underline{\mathsf{CMnd}}\to\operatorname{Cat}

sending
-
to
\Delta_{\infty}^{op},
where the induced strict monoidal functor

\Delta_{+}^{op}\to\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta_{\infty}^{op},\Delta_{\infty}^{op}\right)

encodes the action. Using the nerve construction, this can be regarded as an enriched functor

\mathbb{W}:\underline{\mathsf{CMnd}}\to\underline{\mathrm{Set}}^{\Delta^{op}}

of simplicial categories. A
homotopy coherent comonad
on a quasicategory
\mathscr{D}
is a simplicially enriched functor

\overline{J}:\underline{\mathsf{CMnd}}\to\underline{\mathsf{QCat}}

which sends
-
to
\mathscr{D}.
This is the same data as a strict map of simplicial monoids

N\left(\Delta_{+}^{op}\right)\to\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right).

The
\infty
-category
\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)
can be modeled by the quasicategory obtained as the weighted limit
\left\{\mathbb{W},J\right\}
in the simplicially enriched category
\underline{\mathsf{QCat}}.
It has the following universal property:

For any quasicategory
\mathcal{K},
there is a canonical isomorphism of simplicial sets

\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathcal{K},\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)\right)\cong\mathsf{Nat}_{\Delta}\left(\mathbb{W}\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right),\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathcal{K},\overline{J}\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)\right)\right),

where the right hand side is the simplicial set of simplicially enriched natural transformations of simplicial functors

\underline{\mathsf{CMnd}}\to\underline{\mathrm{Set}}^{\Delta^{op}}.

This universal property allows a direct description of the simplicial set
\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right),
by letting
\mathcal{K}=\Delta^{n},
and taking
0
-simplices of functor quasicategories. Unwinding the definitions, an
n
-simplex of
\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)
can be described as a map

\sigma:\Delta_{\infty}^{op}\to\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta^{n},\mathscr{D}\right)

such that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\Delta_{+}^{op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta^{n},\overline{J}\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{W}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta^{n},\mathscr{D}\right),\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta^{n},\mathscr{D}\right)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)\circ\sigma}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta_{+}^{op},\Delta_{+}^{op}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma\circ\left(\mspace{3mu}\cdot\mspace{3mu}\right)}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta_{\infty}^{op},\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta^{n},\mathscr{D}\right),\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta^{n},\mathscr{D}\right)\right).}

The comonad
J
is encoded as a strict map of simplicial monoids

\overline{J}:\Delta_{+}^{op}\to\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right).

For a given object
D
in
\mathscr{D},
we get by composition a functor

\mathsf{ev}_{D}\circ\overline{J}:\Delta_{+}^{op}\to\mathscr{D}.

Unwinding the definitions above even further, the structure of a
J
-coalgebra on
D
is a lift

\textstyle{\Delta_{+}^{op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{ev}_{D}\circ\overline{J}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{\infty}^{op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}

such that if
\beta=\left(D=A_{0}\to JD=A_{1}\right),
then (the
1
-skeletal image of)
A
can be described as

\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{JD\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{D}}
\scriptstyle{J\left(\beta\right)}
\textstyle{J^{2}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{J\left(\epsilon_{D}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{JD}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{JD}}
\scriptstyle{J\left(\Delta_{D}\right)}
\scriptstyle{J^{2}\left(\beta\right)}
\textstyle{J^{3}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{J^{2}D}}
\scriptstyle{J\left(\epsilon_{JD}\right)}
\scriptstyle{J^{2}\left(\epsilon_{D}\right)}

Notice that the diagram without the dotted arrows is precisely
\mathsf{ev}_{D}\circ\overline{J},
and the dotted arrows are provided by the lift. We stress that one needs the full diagram, not just
\beta,
as the lift also includes higher simplices that provide coherence data for the algebraic relations needed for the above to describe a homotopy coherent functor of
\infty
-categories
\Delta_{\infty}^{op}\to\mathscr{D}.
Nonetheless, we will often denote a
J
-coalgebra informally as

\beta:D\to JD.

Remark 13.19
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be an
\infty
-category and let
J
be a comonad on
\mathscr{D}.
Then composition with
J
induces a comonad
J_{*}
on
\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathcal{K},\mathscr{D}\right).
Unwinding the definitions, one has a canonical isomorphism of simplicial sets

\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J_{*}}\left(\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathcal{K},\mathscr{D}\right)\right)\cong\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathcal{K},\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)\right).

Applying this to
\mathcal{K}=\Delta^{1}
implies that for
\left(X,\beta\right)
and
\left(X^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\right)

J
-coalgebras, we have a pullback diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(\left(X,\beta\right),\left(X^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\right)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J_{*}}\left(\mathscr{D}^{\Delta^{1}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ast\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\left(X,\beta\right),\left(X^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\right)\right)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right).}

This means an
n
-simplex of
\mathbb{M}\mathrm{ap}_{\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)}\left(\left(X,\beta\right),\left(X^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\right)\right)
can be identified with an
n
-simplex of
\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J_{*}}\left(\mathscr{D}^{\Delta^{1}}\right)
which upon evaluation and
0
and
1
is a degenerate
n
-simple on
\left(X,\beta\right)
and
\left(X^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\right)
respectively. But an
n
-simplex of
\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J_{*}}\left(\mathscr{D}^{\Delta^{1}}\right)
is the same data as a
J_{*}
-coalgebra in
\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1},\mathscr{D}\right).
This is the same as a functor

\Delta_{\infty}^{op}\to\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1},\mathscr{D}\right)

which is pointwise a
J
-coalgebra. This is the same as an
n
-simplex in

\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta_{\infty}^{op},\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta^{1},\mathscr{D}\right)\right)
which is pointwise a
J
-coalgebra, and that is the same as an
n
-simplex in
\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta^{1},\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta_{\infty}^{op},\mathscr{D}\right)\right)
which takes values in
J
-coalgebras. It follows that
\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)
is a full subcategory of
\operatorname{Fun}\left(\Delta_{\infty}^{op},\mathscr{D}\right).

Theorem 13.20
.

Let
L\dashv R
exhibit
\mathscr{C}
as a coreflective subcategory of
\mathscr{D}.
Denote the co-unit of this adjunction by
\varepsilon.
Let
J
be a comonad on
\mathscr{D}
such that for all
n,

RJ\left(\varepsilon_{J^{n}LX}\right)
is an equivalence. Then there is a canonical structure on the functor
RJL
of a comonad on
\mathscr{C}
and there is a pullback diagram

\textstyle{\mathbf{CoAlg}_{RJL}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U_{RJL}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U_{J}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}.}

Proof.

By the proof of Lemma
13.17
,
Q_{1}\left(J\right)=\left(RJL,J,\varepsilon_{JL}\right)
is a comonoid object of
\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right),
and since
i_{0}^{*}
is monoidal,

RJL=i_{0}^{*}Q_{1}\left(J\right)

is a comonad on
\mathscr{C}.
By the proof of the same lemma, the map

CJC\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C\left(\epsilon_{C}\right)}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}C

is a morphism of comonads, where
\epsilon
is the co-unit of
J
. Thus there is an induced functor

\mathbf{CoAlg}_{CJC}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)\to\mathbf{CoAlg}_{C}\left(\mathscr{D}\right).

This implies that if
\beta:X\to CJCX
is a co-algebra for
CJC,
that the composite

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{CJCX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C\left(\epsilon_{CX}\right)}
\textstyle{CX}

is an a co-algebra for
C.
But
C
is an idempotent comonad, so this implies that the dotted arrow is an equivalence. It follows that the underlying object of any
CJC
co-algebra lies in the essential image of
C,
or equivalently, the essential image of
L.
(More precisely, a
CJC
co-algebra has higher coherency data involving higher powers of
CJC,
and each of these maps to higher powers of
C
in a coherent way, exhibiting each
CJC
-coalgebra as a
C
-coalgebra.)

Notice that we have a commutative diagram (up to canonical homotopy)

\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{RJL}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{CJC}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D},}

since

\displaystyle CJC\circ L

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle CJLRL

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle CJL

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle L\circ RJL

as
RL\simeq id_{\mathscr{C}}.

Since
L
is fully faithful, this implies that
CJC
restricts to
RJL
on the subcategory
\mathscr{C},
and thus
\mathbf{CoAlg}_{RJL}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)
is equivalent to the full subcategory of
\mathbf{CoAlg}_{CJC}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)
on those in the essential image of
\mathscr{C},
and this equivalence is realized at the level of objects by sending a
RJL
-coalgebra
X
in
\mathscr{C}
to
LX.
But we’ve already seen that all
CJC
-coalgebras have their underling object in the essential image of
L.
Thus we have commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathbf{CoAlg}_{RJL}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U_{RJL}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbf{CoAlg}_{CJC}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U_{CJC}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbf{CoAlg}_{C}{\mathscr{D}}.}

Since we also have a map of comonads
CJC\to J,
there is an induced functor

\rho:\mathbf{CoAlg}_{CJC}\left(\mathscr{D}\right)\to\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right).

Informally, given any
CJC
-coalgebra
\beta:X\to CJCX,
the composite

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\to}}CJCX\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon_{JCX}}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}JCX\stackrel{{\scriptstyle J\left(\varepsilon_{X}\right)}}{{-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu-\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\mskip-3.0mu\longrightarrow}}JX

exhibits
X
as a
J
-coalgebra. Combining this observation with the functor
\rho,
we conclude that if

X\to RJLX

is an
RJL
-coalgebra, then

LX\to JLX

is a
J
-coalgebra where the latter map is induced by the adjunction
L\dashv R.
In particular, there is a functor

\tilde{\rho}:\mathbf{CoAlg}_{RJL}\left(\mathscr{C}\right)\to\mathbf{CoAlg}_{J}\left(\mathscr{D}\right).

We will now show the converse, namely, if

\beta:LX\to JLX

then the adjoint map

\tilde{\beta}:X\to RJLX

is an
RJL
-coalgebra. Recall that
Q_{1}\left(J\right)
is a comonoid object in
\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right).
Encode this by a strict map of simplicial monoids

\mathbb{J}:\Delta_{+}^{op}\to\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta}^{1}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right).

Then
i_{0}^{*}\circ\mathbb{J}=:\overline{RJL}
and
i_{1}^{*}\circ\mathbb{J}=:\overline{J}
encode the comonad structure of
RJL
and
J
respectively. Moreover, it follows from Remark
13.16
that for any object
X
of
\mathscr{C},
the diagram

\textstyle{\Delta_{+}^{op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{J}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{J}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{1}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Fun}_{\Delta^{1}}\left(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{D}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{ev}_{LX}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Fun}\left(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{ev}_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}}
.

commutes up to a canonical homotopy

\mu_{X}:\mathsf{ev}_{X}\circ i_{0}^{*}\circ\mathbb{J}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}R\circ\mathsf{ev}_{LX}\circ i_{1}^{*}\circ\mathbb{J}.

Suppose that

B:\Delta^{op}_{\infty}\to\mathcal{D}

encodes the
J
-coalgebra on
LX,
with

\beta=\left(B_{0}=LX\to B_{1}=JLX\right).

Then

B|_{\Delta_{+}^{op}}=\mathsf{ev}_{LX}\circ i_{1}^{*}\circ\mathbb{J}.

It follows that

\mu_{X}:\mathsf{ev}_{X}\circ i_{0}^{*}\circ\mathbb{J}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\Rightarrow}}\left(R\circ B\right)|_{\Delta^{op}_{+}}.

Since
\mu_{X}
is component-wise an equivalence in
\mathscr{C},
there exists a lift

\textstyle{\Delta_{+}^{op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{ev}_{X}\circ i_{0}^{\ast}\circ\mathbb{J}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{\infty}^{op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}

and an equivalence

\widetilde{\mu}_{X}:A\to R\circ B

whose components are the same as

and are the vertical maps in the following diagram, which commutes up to canonical coherent homotopies, with the dashed arrows indicating the parts of the diagram
A
which are not included in
\mathsf{ev}_{X}\circ i_{0}^{*}\circ\mathbb{J}
:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}}

\sim

\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\beta}}
\textstyle{RJLX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{\prime}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta^{\prime}_{X}}
\textstyle{RJL\left(RJLX\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{1}=RJ\left(\varepsilon_{JLX}\right)}

\sim

\scriptstyle{RJL\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{X}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{\prime}_{RJLX}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{RJLX}}
\scriptstyle{RJL\left(\Delta^{\prime}_{X}\right)}
\textstyle{\left(RJL\right)^{3}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\cdots}

\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{\prime}_{RJLRJLX}}

\scriptstyle{R\left(\epsilon_{LX}\right)}

\scriptstyle{RJ\left(\beta\right)}
\textstyle{RJ^{2}LX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{RJ\left(\epsilon_{LX}\right)}
\scriptstyle{R\left(\epsilon_{JLX}\right)}
\scriptstyle{R\left(\Delta_{JD}\right)}
\scriptstyle{RJ\left(\Delta_{LX}\right)}
\scriptstyle{RJ^{2}\left(\beta\right)}
\textstyle{RJ^{3}LX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\cdots}

where

and

are the co-unit and co-multiplication of

and

and

are the co-unit and co-multiplication of

The notation
\mu_{n}
refers to the proof of Lemma
13.15
. The first dashed arrow is
R\left(\beta\right)\circ\eta_{X},
which is
\tilde{\beta}
by definition. We will inductively build the desired lift by defining a lift

\textstyle{\left(\Delta_{+}\right)_{\leq n}^{op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{ev}_{X}\circ i_{0}^{\ast}\circ\mathbb{J}}

\textstyle{\left(\Delta_{\infty}\right)_{\leq n}^{op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
